[05/02/24 02:05 AM] Megan Valentine: +Sharing these notes from my meditation retreat today ❤ 



The full range of emotions
During this retreat you have been exploring and playing with emotions. While during meditation you have been playing with pleasure, forgiveness and relaxation, we want you to also have a broader perspective on the full range of emotions (fear, sadness, joy, anger, grief etc….).

All emotions are a homeostasis or feedback mechanism. Each emotion is a different form of wisdom providing us with important information about our experience. While some emotions might feel better, it is critical we listen to all of them. 

Many of us have developed patterns where we suppress or do not fully feel our emotions. We have developed this pattern because during development we were told that it is bad to express certain emotions. To truly hear the wisdom of our emotions we have to allow them to move through our system. Our negative relationship with some emotions (sadness or anger) comes not from the emotion itself, but our resistance to feeling it. 

A powerful way to check in with our emotions is by asking ourselves “How much am I bracing vs how much am I embracing?”. When we brace, we are resisting our experience. This resistance creates internal conflict and does not allow the emotion to run its course.  “That which we resist persists”. One of the primary ways we brace is by intellectualizing or telling a story. Operating at this higher level of abstraction means we are not allowing ourselves to feel. Embracing on the other hand, allows the emotion to move through us. When we embrace 1) our story changes and 2) we have a feeling of release.
Emotional Arcs
There are common emotional arcs that we may pass through. This emotional arc often starts with “negative” emotions and then releases into “positive” emotions. Each of these emotions feels quite different in the body.
Anger - Can be a very intense emotion. Shows that you care. Built by repeatedly repressing. Often has fear underlying it.
Fear - Is connected to future uncertainty. Involves losing something you care about. Results from potential grief in the future.
Grief - Often related to loss of something you care about. 
Joy - “The matriarch of emotions, will not be present when all of her children aren’t welcome”
Serenity - Calmness and ease. A sense of having enough
Peace - Harmony. Often happens with a deeper understanding.
Stages of Emotional Development
Repression - We receive feedback that is not good to feel certain emotions. We start to repress them in response to this feedback. 
Management - As we get older we start interacting with them at a more intellectual level. We try to manage them and reason with them. For a lot of people this is where they are. People find they are controlling their emotions but are not happy. Controlling means not just the negative ones but also the positive ones. 
Flowing - Instead of managing the emotions we allow them to flow and feel them deeply. 
Unification - All emotions have a similar tinge to them. 
 
When we have more pleasure available it is easier to look at things we might otherwise be avoiding. We can use this resource to help us navigate difficulty. In some ways this retreat is all about falling in love with the feeling you have been avoiding.iI/

I so feel your love and care for me. I haven’t experienced that before. We have something beautiful, special, and profound. That poem resonates with how I feel about you. 

I was too tired and still processing to pinpoint and express what I was feeling last night so want to share a few reflections: 

I’m still wounded and trying to heal that while navigating a ship through a hurricane with work. I’m trying with all my capacity to navigate this storm with integrity while putting you first without losing or compromising my centered self. It’s hard and there are things I can do better. There is anxious and masculine energy that accumulates when I’m in the hurricane. I have the tools/awareness to softly unwind that and glide back into my calm/feminine self but it requires some space and last night felt like jumping from the hurricane into a completely different context without a moment to transition my nervous system.  

I cannot overstate how much I value, appreciate and love your honesty and sincerity. I need that level of open communication to feel comfortable jumping all in on intimacy and am so glad you shared what you did in bed last night. Some of my tears of sadness related to you saying that at dinner you wanted me to be wittier and more connected. I felt sad because being witty in that particular setting where I don’t feel intimacy or know the people well isn’t who I am. I’m a bit weird, I have a unique ability to connect with people at a deep, real, and genuine level. But it’s not always immediate and I don’t do that well in a large group setting. 

It’s really important to me to feel at ease in situations like last night which requires you to trust the people you care about will see all of me given a bit of time. I experienced the neg as you being insecure with the version they were seeing in the moment which did make me feel small. It doesn’t relate to self worth as I’m secure there, but rather to a feeling that I wasn’t enough for you in that moment, and a need I have is to feel I’m enough with my partner.

I’m grateful it happened and we were able to talk about it. There’s nothing to apologize about. I feel closer to you as my person today and my love for you is so strong. I’m going to miss you this week and am looking forward to sweet thoughts about you in nature. I love you ❤️iI

[08/28/24 02:07 AM] Jordan: +_I’m sorry to hear that man :/ I hope she is feeling better. First thing, this is super common. Probably the most common thing I get called about and see patients for outside of high blood pressure and high cholesterol. Few questions: 

1) How fast was her heart rate
2) what did the urgent care do / order
3) age and other health history 
4) how is she feeling today 

The two tenets of treat afib are 1) symptoms and 2) stroke risk, which can be scary but fortunately the risk is very well mitigated with anticoagulation (like <1-2% per year). 

So in terms of treatment, those are the things we focus oniIi

[08/28/24 02:32 AM] Jordan: +7To be honest, if I were 70 with any evidence of afib, regardless of the “calculator” I’d probably be on a blood thinner. Kinda like a personal experience and new data thing. 

But again, not urgent. The risk is very low annually. So it’s ok to be patient. Lots of people are very uncomfortable with a blood thinner and so in that setting a monitor that you wear for a month or so can be helpful because you might find out she is in afib a lot more than she even realizes. 

If she wants a middle ground, for now she could take a baby aspirin to make her feel better about the stroke risk, but the data is weak for that. In my opinion, probably doesn’t hurt until you figure more out. And for whatever reason, people are more comfortable with a baby aspirin. So it’s like a middle ground that patients appreciateiI+

[08/28/24 10:04 PM] Jordan: +Hey - just got back and have a couple meetings. I can come over a bit later and bring you some souvla if that sounds good? Also I just chatted with Mchale and took the notes below (it’s kind a lot but I just verbatim tried to write what he said)iI
[08/28/24 10:04 PM] Jordan: +1 month is completely fine - what’ll happen is there’s a calculator to determine someone’s stroke risk, it’s medicine, more and more data that all of the factors in the calculator are weighted equally, age biggest predictor of stroke risk, stroke risk is low 1-5% when they get diagnosed with afib, her calc. Risk is prob 1.2/12 so low risk. My guess is the seizures not related to this if she’s had brain imagery, if the imagery showed evidence of small strokes then she should definitely be on a blood thinner, she already has some evidence she’s been having strokes. 

Other half of afib is synmptoms. Million people who live their whole life and never know they have afib and we diagnose after stroke. We don’t need to put the people who haven’t had stroke on medication because they’re asymptomatic, the reason she went to urgent care is light headed and dizzy, you have to treat the symptoms, people feel it and get light headed because they’re normally in a normal rhythm and they flip and they feel it really fast. In her case, the dose of the metricol (?) is very low 12.5 is lowest dose, we’ll start that four times a day until people are on 50 or 100 twice a day, lot of room to go up if she’s symptomatic, other thing is metricol is most basic medication and there’s 15 medications we use let’s say we decide she’s on anti collagulation because of stroke risk age etc and we’re trying to manage the symptoms can take a while to get them under control until you find medication that works and even after that you can have procedure can have ablation for afib, meaning lot of options. What cardiologist will prob. do is put a monitor on her, she went in there yesterday and was light headed, maybe it’s afib maybe her blood pressure is low, you put a monitor on her chest and when she feels light headed that’s on a phone and it pairs the symptom with whatever her heart rhythm is doing after a month we look at that and we see whether symptoms are correlated, if they’re not then we focus outside of afib, if they are and we fix and stabilize afib then we fix it. Everyone is afraid of bruising - we can always give someone blood but if they have a stroke, cardiologists tend to lean on anti-coagulation even if it makes people nervous.iI

[09/10/24 05:27 PM] Megan Valentine: +Just got off the second opinion call and Dr. Li was super kind. He agrees with Dr. Chang about for sure needing the surgery and thinks I should do it in the next few months (he thought January was okay). Once it starts blocking fluid I’d get very sick, so much much better to do it before that happens. He thinks there’s some chance the tension headaches/memory gaps/brain fog is caused by the tumor even though the mri doesn’t show fluid backing up, and would push the surgery earlier if these things were getting worse. He described the risks as a bit higher than Dr chang. 10-30% chance of short term neurological issues (<3 months), 1-5% chance of long term issues, 1-2% chance of infection, <1% chance of issues with anesthesia. The neurological issues he mentioned were around balance/swallowing/limb control. He described himself as a more conservative surgeon - less likely to operate than Dr Chang which might also mean his risk assessment is a bit higher. He said Dr Chang is a very very good surgeon. He said there’s only one surgical approach to removing a tumor from the 4th ventricle, suboccipital craniotomy, so he agreed with Dr Chang there as well. He said I’d be able to shower/use the restroom by myself but would need someone to cook/drive/lift things for me for a few weeks. I forgot to ask about stairs.  He thinks there’s some low chance it is an ependymoma and if it wasn’t fully removed I might need radiation (but unlikely)iI

[11/27/24 04:13 AM] Jordan: +Other recommended things/comments - (1) Coffee tour at Monarch Coffee Farm. It’s driving distance from the Fairmont. You can do other ones but we really liked that one. (2) This is only going to work if you have a car to drive there, but this beach - Mahai’ula Beach - it is truly unreal. You hike through this like pure volcanic ash and to a secluded set of beaches. It’s a REALLY rough drive for like 2-3 miles so your car could get beat up, ideally you’d have an SUV. Will send a picture here of what it looks like. (3) Star gazing at Mauna Kea was unreal - we ended up doing that as well. (4) Awesome restaurant in Hilo called Turtle and the Moon. (5) Kamuela / Pololu hike is an amazing substitute if my black #4 is closed (which it was last time I was there) iI

[04/19/25 11:37 PM] Megan Valentine: +Cassava-Coconut Flour Loaf (Gluten & Grain-Free)

Ingredients:
•1 cup cassava flour
•1/4 cup coconut flour
•1/2 tsp baking soda
•1/4 tsp salt
•3 large eggs
•1/2 cup milk (dairy or non-dairy)
•1/4 cup oil or melted butter (e.g., coconut oil, olive oil, ghee)
•2 tbsp honey or maple syrup (optional for a slightly sweet loaf)
•1 tsp apple cider vinegar or lemon juice (helps rise and balance flavor)

Optional Add-ins:
•1 tsp cinnamon or herbs (rosemary, thyme, garlic powder for savory)
•1/2 cup shredded coconut, nuts, or seeds
•1 mashed ripe banana for a more cake-like, sweet loaf

⸻

Instructions:
1.Preheat oven to 350°F (175°C). Grease or line a loaf pan with parchment.
2.Mix dry ingredients: In a bowl, whisk together cassava flour, coconut flour, baking soda, and salt.
3.Mix wet ingredients: In another bowl, beat eggs, then add milk, oil, honey/maple syrup, and vinegar.
4.Combine: Add dry to wet ingredients and stir until fully combined. Let it sit for 2–3 minutes to allow coconut flour to absorb liquid.
5.Pour into loaf pan and smooth the top.
6.Bake for 35–45 minutes or until a toothpick comes out clean and the top is golden.
7.Cool in the pan for 10 minutes, then transfer to a wire rack to finish coolingiIH

[06/06/25 05:06 PM] Jordan: +NEngadin Valley / St. Moritz / Sils / Pontresina (Switzerland)
Pros: Very sunny microclimate, more reliable weather than Dolomites, stunning lakes + high mountains + villages that feel Swiss and special. Amazing hut options.

Cons: More expensive.

Weather: Possibly your best bet for sun. The Upper Engadin is known for clear weather.iIN
[06/06/25 05:06 PM] Jordan: +	Berner Oberland — Lauterbrunnen / Wengen / Mürren (Switzerland)
Pros: Possibly the most beautiful alpine scenery in Europe. Great villages. Incredible hiking and hut options.

Cons: Busy. Expensive.

Weather: Less reliable than Engadin but better than Dolomites.iI

[08/28/25 09:49 PM] Jordan: +hYeah I know all of the doctors there. They do a technique called chiropractic bio physics - you can look up that technique. It’s pretty in depth treatment plans to restore the correct degree of curvature in the spine. 

Three sessions a week over three months usually about 7k cost over the first three months. 

They start with an x-ray to measure the curvature at every spinal segment, then they do another X-ray after 3 months of treatment to show you how much the curves changed. 

It’s a good technique. I believe in its value. It helped me a lot with my neck pain because my neck was in reverse curvature. iId

[03/27/26 04:33 PM] Megan Valentine: +￼Bedrooms idea!iI
[03/27/26 09:54 PM] Jordan: +	So I've been pressure-testing the idea with Claude and here's where I landed:

The original concept — an app that monitors everything a worker does on their computer, maps their workflows, and recommends automations — has real problems as a standalone product:

1. The capture layer is getting commoditized fast. Microsoft is building screen-recording AI (Recall) directly into Windows, and Limitless already does passive observation. Building another "watch everything" tool means racing against companies with way more resources.

2. The three use cases I had (help the worker, help the employer, find automations) actually conflict with each other. The moment an employer can use it for surveillance, workers stop trusting it. You can't be someone's productivity ally and their boss's monitoring tool at the same time.

3. Distribution is brutal. Convincing random workers to download an app that watches their entire screen requires a ton of trust for an unclear payoff.

What actually got interesting is a narrower version: use the observation tool as a scoping/diagnostic instrument for an AI consulting practice. Think of it like this — instead of me spending 10 hours manually understanding how a small business operates before I can help them, the software does that ethnographic research automatically. The business installs it for a few days, it watches their workflows, asks clarifying questions, and produces a report of their biggest automation opportunities. Then I show up with a near-finished proposal to implement everything.

This works because:
- The tool isn't the product, it's lead gen. So it doesn't matter if Microsoft commoditizes the capture layer.
- The real value is in the implementation, which is exactly the kind of messy, human, trust-dependent work that AI labs won't do themselves.
- Mark Cuban's point applies — there are millions of businesses that need AI help but have no idea where to start. They're not going to figure it out alone.
- A friend's framework (Evan from South Park Commons) basically argues that low-tech-capability businesses who need hand-holding are the best customers right now because they'll never build it themselves. "The slog is the moat."

So the play is: build a lightweight diagnostic tool, use it to automate the most expensive part of consulting (the discovery phase), and sell implementation. If it works, the long-term upside is either licensing the tool to other consultants or turning the accumulated workflow knowledge into vertical software products.iI
